I think it’s hard for someone who has never lived in a foreign country to understand what a second - or even third - class citizen is like. Everyone sees a good life on social media and assumes that the place would give it to them, but it doesn’t work that way. If you immigrate, you will always be a foreigner. Simple as that. Your children’s children will still be a foreigner, until they marry locals enough in their bloodline to become locals themselves. It’s not only about looks, but what a nation collectively is like as well. You can’t teach someone a national spirit or how to behave, if you aren’t raised like that. And yes, people don’t like someone who is objectively different than them, hence the integration into local communities is a bit hard in practice, given the individualism on the rise as well. 

I was about to argue about America, but then when prof said that it’s American rules, I got called out for my own thinking as well. As someone who was growing up with a ‘globalisation’ concept of the world, it’s a hard truth to swallow that the best place is to stay where I was born if I want status. A day salary for an hour salary, a week worth of day-offs comparing to a month, I do get why people don’t care about their own communities and just leave though. Young people feel that older generation are holding all the wealth and opportunities so there is no room for us to grow and be innovative, hence they leave. Society becomes much more  bureaucratic at this time, and we shall see how it goes.

> What was important to me, was powerful decision makers noticed me and gave me the right spot to prosper and to have fun.  
>   
> Therefore, a lot of what Dr Jiang illuminated on such as finding a clique (kinda like a secret society) to trangress together, to "cheat" the game etc are all very true.   
>   
> That's just what a certain type of people in suits and Hermes ties do, all the time. We created working groups and strategy papers to create a semblance of reasonableness while demoralizing others to impose our will, often what the guy at the top had in mind.  
>   
> Sometimes it's for the "greater good" as profit motives demand the removal of rent seekers and seat warmers.  
>   
> But there are some caveats:  
>   
> 1. You need to identify kindred spirits that you could transgress together with and rig the games to your advantage. Therefore temperamental alignment is very important, in particular, open-mindedness. You need to find the right place and culture too.  
>   
> 2. You still need to be accepted into that clique itself. That's probably the hardest part for most people. Oftentimes the first point of being initiated is to get noticed by "the clique", because sometimes when you're on the outside, you can't be sure who those members of "the clique" are. People wear their masks.  
>   
> 3. That clique must also be predisposed to strong desire to win, which are surprisingly few in today's work environment. You're in the business of praxis to get what you want with the clique.  
>   
> But here's the thing, if you don't transgress and break the game, and instead, you play it safe, your outcome is more likely to be predetermined or capped.  
>   
> But if you take risk, there's an asymmetric  gain from that. And to protect yourself, you should observe and be willing to listen to others gossip to gain informational advantage.  
>   
> Also, if your parents or teachers, or whatever, teach you to be in a certain way to "fit in" or to be "good members" of the society or the community you "belong to", but they themselves are mediocre, low-key miserable or simply unremarkable, they're probably not the people you want to learn from, if winning is what you want.  
>   
> When I was in Asia, I found the locals to be generally quite rigid when it came to social conventions etc, while whinging about being clipped by those norms. Tbh I found them rather immature, irrational, contemptable and boring.   
>   
> The only exceptions I found, were those in the crypto and tech startup sector, where, there was hunger to get ahead.

When Europe-origin immigration to the US was at its peak in the early part of the 20th century, there was talk about how America was being “taken over” by Catholics, the “dirty” Irish & the “criminal” Italians. These groups went on to become successfully assimilated into US society, and over time, as the tumult in Europe subsided, the immigration from those countries came down. Great production, but this narrative is essentially "Great Replacement" theory dressed up in Game Theory jargon. To claim immigration is a "recent accident" or only happened via slavery ignores millennia of human history—from the Silk Road to Ancient Rome, humans have always moved voluntarily for opportunity.

More importantly, the video romanticizes a "natural" past of fixed communities that never actually existed. That "utopia" was defined by plagues, extreme gender violence, and rigid caste hierarchies. What the speaker calls a "trap" is actually the foundation of the modern world: the global exchange of knowledge, labor, and speech that has pulled more people out of poverty than any other system.

By framing assimilation as a "loss" and promoting demographic dominance as the only "winning strategy," this video isn't just analyzing trends—it’s normalizing segregationist logic and undermining the very pluralism that makes a decent life possible in the 21st century.

2:16 African American here and I love my blackness along with my diaspora. Black American and African-American are not the same. Black American refers to the black people in American who can trace their lineage back to the first census. African-American generally refers to Black people who migrated to the United States of America and now have citizenship. I speculate that the term was created to Africanize/Other Black people who have origin in America to prevent them from being able to file a reparations claim from the atrocities of slavery and other harmful events to them in the United States of America. 

I cannot speak for the Latinos and the Hispanics because I’m not as knowledgeable in their cultures yet. 

The reason why my ethnic group has lower economic success than other migrant groups including Caucasians is because since the inception of the United States of America, there were laws and rules in place that specifically prevented us from having access to resources, education, wealth, etc. These types of laws were in existence up until the 1960s. There are millions Black people in the United States of America alive today who were marginalized by these laws. Their descendants and family members are harmed today via the trickle down effect. 

One of many examples of these is the Tignon Law, which required black women in the 1700s to cover their hair in public. 

The effects of these laws still remain today. Although the law has changed, many institutions still assimilate to the identity of those rules. Thanks to elite overproduction (Jiang Xueqin) by the time Black people in America were given access to the ability to make economic progress, all or most of the positions of that would give access to economic and social mobility were filled and dominated by people who once enslaved us (what a coincidence). 

Due to creativity, skill, hard work, talent, etc many Black people in America have been able to breakthrough these barriers and become highly successful.

We are not lazy, we are not stupid, we are not less than any other group. Historical documentation shows that we were intentionally given lower quality education along with religious, psychosis, and physical, emotional and mental abuse from the United States government.

Sources: “Caste: The Origins of Our Discontents” and “The Warmth of Other Suns” by Isabel Wilkerson. “Native Son” by Richard Wright. “Where Do We Go from Here: Chaos or Community?” by Martin Luther King Jr.

I want people to pay close attention to Professor Jiang's ideology in this lecture, and notice massive holes in his thought process, and what he stands for.



Ironically, professor Jiang is teaching from a eurocentric individualistic standpoint. The only reason why he is who he is today is because of a Western education, and the Western ethos of self-expression and individualism. He's actually quite open about his troubled past, his depression, and how he found balance later on in his life. Ironically, this is a type of self-expression is only found in Western culture. Eastern culture is more private about their personal struggles and prefer to maintain the status quo at the expense of their own well-being. 



In this particular video, he's basically teaching his students to break the rules, be more confident, be more individualistic, and ignore accountability; he's basically teaching the students to be more American. Which is quite coincidental because he's so critical of America and its declining global influence, per his own analysis.



Professor Jiang is highly reductive and overly simplistic about immigration, cultural identity, intelligence, and collectivism versus individualism.



But the reality is far from his game theory. The world is more codependent than ever before; cultures and racial identities have become more homogeneous, and there isn't one state actor who will become dominant, nor will there be one racial group that's predominantly more intelligent than another racial group.



A perfect example is if America went to war with Iran, and Iran shut down the straight of Hormuz, the East Asian economy would collapse in a day. If China stopped producing American goods, American economy would collapse in a day. If America barred all technological advancements both from an academic or commercial standpoint from entering into China, academia and technological innovations would severely stagnate in China. China has a rich history of intellectual property theft from the US. The Chinese cannot innovate on their own without stealing from the US. He talks about breaking the rules, but China has been breaking the rules for decades. 


The world is far more codependent than these reductive categories of intelligence per racial groups, or which racial group procreates more, or which racial group breaks the rules more or is more confident.



Finally it should be obvious to everyone that professor Jiang is pro-China, whether it's from an ideological governmental perspective, from a nationalist perspective, or from a material perspective by making money teaching students from a Western perspective or from donations/subscriptions. Professor Jiang wants the United States to fail, he wants China to dominate globally. Not once is Professor Jiang ever critical of China's own policies. He would probably celebrate China's IP theft, because it supports his: Breaking the rules because it's not "their game" theory.  Let's talk about the elephant in the room, why hasn't professor Jiang ever mentioned the fact that Mao starved millions of his own people to death? This happened in the late 50s and early '60s, my dad was born before the great famine in China, this is a very recent event. For all his criticism of the United States and its horrible global policies, the difference is that in the United States we can express ourselves against such horrible policies, but you can't say the same in China. Why doesn't he ever discuss the lack of accountability both of an American standpoint but also from a Chinese standpoint about COVID and its lab leak? This is what happens when you teach the next generation of wealthy students about being rule breakers, you're basically teaching the elite, which these students represent, how to take shortcuts in life and face as little accountability as possible, yet the common person is the one who will experience all the hardships from these rule breaking decision makers. Don't be fooled by professor Jiang making these videos for free for the masses, they don't apply to the common person. The actual people he's marketing to are Chinese elites who will make decisions in the future. And he's basically teaching elites how to dodge accountability and become a dominant force in the world. Just like the typical American elite.

Hi Professor, I found the lecture interesting and wanted to share a couple of thoughts. When you discussed how dating can be more difficult for Asian men and Black women, you emphasized income as a factor for Asian men but didn’t address physical attractiveness. I think attractiveness also plays a role, while there are certainly attractive Asian men and Black women, those who are perceived as less attractive may face greater difficulty finding partners, especially compared to groups that have larger dating pools. Less attractive White men and women also exist, but they often date within their own ethnicity, which may provide more options.

I was also a bit confused by the shift from discussing ethnicity to focusing specifically on religion, particularly Islam. My understanding is that higher birth rates among Muslims are more closely related to cultural and religious values, especially among immigrants who tend to practice their faith more strictly after leaving their country of origin. This often includes prioritizing family over material success. In that sense, this is similar to many Hispanic families in the United States, who may value having a family over accumulating wealth or status.

In contrast, many White Americans may prioritize material comfort, such as owning a nice home, new cars, or maintaining a certain lifestyle, which can lead to having fewer children or none at all. From my perspective, Muslims and Hispanic communities would likely have larger families regardless of where they live, including in Western countries. This reflects their values rather than an intention to replace another culture, ethnicity, or religion.

Everything stated here is mostly true, but there are major flaws. These flaws do not necessarily change the final conclusions, but they significantly affect how the situation is understood.

First, counting Muslims as a parallel category to ethnicity is incorrect. Muslims are already included within other ethnic groups in the same datasets. Islam is a religion, not an ethnicity , Muslims can belong to any ethnic background. Therefore, treating “Muslims” as a separate ethnic group leads to double counting and distorted conclusions.

Second, the claim that European immigrants are primarily Muslim is misleading. Most immigrants to Europe come from South Asia and other regions, and they include both Muslims and non-Muslims.

Third, the idea that Muslims are inherently difficult to integrate is also inaccurate. Muslim migration to Europe is not new; in many countries it goes back several generations. In fact, many third-generation Muslim communities have largely lost their original cultural practices and are already integrated into European society.

By contrast, Chinatowns exist in almost every major city in Europe and the United States, and some have existed for decades , sometimes close to a century , while maintaining strong cultural separation. This demonstrates that cultural preservation and separation are not uniquely Muslim phenomena.

Claims regarding crime and education are also often oversimplified or selectively framed. East Asian organized crime groups, for example, are well-documented, large-scale, and often more structured and aggressive than commonly acknowledged. A visit to cities like Los Angeles makes this reality evident.

Finally, there is a deeper ideological contradiction that is often ignored. Western culture is, at its core, deeply influenced by Eastern particularly Middle Eastern traditions. Today, Western societies face a dilemma:

1-Becoming more Christian would mean becoming more Middle Eastern or Eastern European in cultural terms,

2- becoming more liberal moves them further away from their historical roots.

One critical factor that is rarely discussed ,and explains many current “European problems” , is the internal struggle between Western Europe and Eastern Europe. In reality, Western Europe opened its borders not only for humanitarian reasons, but also to compete with Eastern European labor.

Western Europe does not want Slavic populations to fully integrate. Instead, it often prefers visibly different groups ,Black, Indian, Arab ,because they are easier to identify, marginalize, and ultimately remove if necessary. Slavs, by contrast, integrate too well and compete too directly.

### **@digilux4017** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=35HRPLVyF0g&lc=UgwdPDgwZmxfM2zUBBV4AaABAg)

Thanks for these lectures you share for the world. One comment though:

The implicit logic in the lecture appears to be that PISA scores function as a proxy for national human capital, and that people migrate toward places where the expected payoff from human capital is highest. In simplified form, the reasoning is that stronger education systems produce stronger economies, and therefore rational actors move toward those centers, which is why the United States becomes the “attractor” in the global system.

The difficulty with this explanation is that it conflates two very different levels of analysis: educational input indicators and societal outcome structures.

Norway typically performs around the OECD average in PISA. It is not among the global top performers in these rankings. If Jiang’s framework were sufficient, one would expect Norway to be relatively unattractive as a destination, since its measured “human capital signal” would appear weaker than that of several East Asian or European systems.

Empirically, however, Norway consistently ranks at the top of global indices measuring quality of life, institutional trust, social mobility, economic stability, and human development. Many migrants choose Norway precisely because the institutional environment produces predictable life outcomes, even if the country does not dominate educational rankings.

In other words, the payoff function individuals maximize is not simply expected income derived from human capital. It is something closer to:

Expected utility = income potential + institutional stability + security + quality of life − migration cost.

In this broader framework, institutional quality can compensate for moderate educational test performance. Norway’s combination of strong institutions, social trust, legal predictability, and low systemic risk creates a different equilibrium from the U.S. “high-competition innovation hub” model.

The deeper issue is therefore methodological. PISA measures an input variable within the education system, while migration decisions respond to output characteristics of entire societies. Treating one as a proxy for the other produces an analytically misleading narrative.

Norway demonstrates that a country can sit around the OECD average in PISA while still being one of the most attractive places in the world to live. That empirical reality suggests that global migration patterns are shaped less by educational rankings than by the broader architecture of institutions and social systems.

### **@jjwmacdo** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=35HRPLVyF0g&lc=UgyHBbmI45eeCKFmhQh4AaABAg)

@21:30 I think there is an incorrect message, but this could be "game theory" semantics. Basically Professor Jiang states "Immigrants will always lose." I would say that the nation will gain more than the immigrant, but the immigrant will also gain. So if you define "winning" as one side gains more than the other, then he's correct. But in terms of economics there are often optimal solutions which are asymmetric. Consider a trading relationship or a business transaction where both parties benefit. Party A is to benefit more that party B, party B can extract some rent from A such that A will avoid the opportunity cost. 
This often the case with immigration.

> **@LimeDude9999**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=35HRPLVyF0g&lc=UgyHBbmI45eeCKFmhQh4AaABAg.ATwePsNoJjfATxDmWkcPQ0)  
>
> Depends on the status of the individuals.  
> Party B is in favor because they directly benefit from the immigration of party A  
> Party C doesn’t directly benefit from party A because they don’t own a home to rent to party A or their own business to profit from A economically.  
> Party C just sees the byproduct of 2x as many people fighting to rent Party B’s room for rent and party B seeing this supply and demand dynamic shift and utilizing the opportunity to increase their gains making things worse for A&C.  
>   
> The only people in favor of unregulated immigration are the ones that directly benefit from it.

### **@АлмазКасенов-г8м** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=35HRPLVyF0g&lc=UgzptqOCKETe7TV5YN54AaABAg)

The video mixes real statistics with very questionable conclusions.



First, the income comparison between ethnic groups ignores **immigration selection bias**. Many Indian immigrants to the U.S. arrive through highly selective visa programs for engineers, doctors, and students, while many migrants from other regions arrive through different labor channels. Comparing these groups as if they represent entire populations is misleading.



Second, using PISA scores to predict which civilizations will dominate the future is a huge stretch. PISA measures specific academic skills, not innovation, leadership, political stability, or economic power. Several countries with excellent PISA scores are not global powers.



Third, the lecture repeatedly suggests genetic explanations for group differences without presenting any serious scientific evidence. Most research attributes outcome differences to socioeconomic factors such as education access, migration policy, historical inequality, and discrimination.



Fourth, the claim that Asian men must earn hundreds of thousands of dollars more to compete in dating markets comes from old dating-app data that cannot represent real-world relationships. Using a single platform’s algorithmic data to make sweeping conclusions about society is not rigorous.



Finally, the “game theory strategy” argument about groups simply maximizing births while avoiding integration misunderstands how migration and assimilation actually work. Historically, second-generation immigrants usually adopt the language, culture, and economic patterns of the societies they grow up in, and fertility rates tend to converge toward the national average.



Overall, the video presents interesting topics, but it often moves from data to conclusions that are far more ideological than scientific.

I'm currently halfway through the video, and I have a question. I understand the issue of having unequal opportunities when it comes to employment and advancement, but I'm not really sure how one tackles dating. 

People prefer to date others often for entirely arbitrary reasons. There are people who are disabled, old, short, whatever, who basically struggle with the same thing as Asian men. I have heard many accounts of older women (as well as men) who feel entirely invisible in the dating pool due to their age. I feel like dating is fundamentally often unfair. You can't force people to date somebody they don't want to, so what are you supposed to do?

In my view, people don't necessarily choose a partner out of rationality. Sometimes it really is just stereotypes, or because they "think" that this race, ethnicity, gender, is hard working, or submissive, or whatever. Sometimes, it's just symbolism. I doubt many people are that thoughtful about why they are dating in the first place, though. Just food for thought.

This analysis is fundamentally flawed. Any discussion of early American development and immigration that glosses over the transatlantic slave trade is not an omission, it is a distortion. Enslaved Africans were central to the economic foundation of the early state, and excluding that reality while advancing arguments about labour, population, or necessity is indefensible. It reads as deliberate historical sanitisation rather than serious scholarship. For a channel that presents itself as academic, this comes across as intellectually dishonest and uncomfortably aligned with right wing propaganda rather than evidence based history.

The same applies to any analysis of modern outcomes. You cannot examine trends affecting Black Americans while ignoring Jim Crow laws and the wider legal, economic, and institutional systems that deliberately imposed long term disadvantage. Stripping that context away is not neutrality, it is misrepresentation.

Simply referring to Black Americans or the wider Black diaspora as “Blacks” is problematic and has clear racist undertones.

Hey can you do a video on 
The Tiananmen Square massacre refers to the bloody crackdown on pro-democracy protesters by the Chinese government on June 3–4, 1989, following weeks of demonstrations that began after the death of reformist leader Hu Yaobang in April 1989. 

Events and Crackdown
Martial law was declared on May 20, 1989, and by the early morning hours of June 4, troops and tanks moved into central Beijing, engaging in violent clashes with demonstrators along Chang'an Avenue and in the Muxidi suburbs, rather than solely within the square itself.  The government labeled the movement a "counter-revolutionary rebellion," and the crackdown resulted in the suppression of the protests, the exile or imprisonment of leaders, and the purge of officials like Zhao Ziyang. 

Casualty Estimates and Aftermath
The death toll remains disputed:

Official CCP tally: 241 dead. 
Independent estimates: Ranging from 300 to 2,700 dead. 
Western diplomats and media: Estimated hundreds to thousands killed, with up to 10,000 arrested.
